FlashAttention online softmax: For each row i (query): m_ij = max(m_{i,j-1}, rowmax(S_ij)) P_ij = exp(S_ij - m_ij) // actually not exactly... Actually the correct algorithm is: - For each row i: m_new = max(m_old, max(S_ij)) exp_scale = exp(m_old - m_new) l_new = l_old * exp_scale + sum(exp(S_ij - m_new)) O_new = O_old * exp_scale + P_ij @ Vj where P_ij = exp(S_ij - m_new) Wait, O_old needs to be rescaled by exp(m_old - m_new) because the denominator changes. More precisely: - We maintain O_unnorm and sum_exp - Or we maintain O_norm = O_unnorm / l_old, but then updating is: O_new = (O_old * l_old * exp(m_old - m_new) + P_ij_unnorm @ Vj) / l_new FlashAttention uses: m_new = max(m_old, rowmax(S)) l_new = l_old * exp(m_old - m_new) + rowsum(exp(S - m_new)) O_new = O_old * exp(m_old - m_new) + matmul(exp(S - m_new), V) Later: O_final = O_new / l_new Wait, actually in FlashAttention-2 they do: m_new = max(m_old, rowmax(S)) P = exp(S - m_new) l_new = l_old * exp(m_old - m_new) + rowsum(P) O_new = O_old * exp(m_old - m_new) + P @ V After all tiles: O = O_new / l_new This requires O_old to be updated with the scaling factor. Actually, a simpler async approach: load data using `__pipeline_memcpy_async` or the PTX: ``` cp.async.ca.shared.global [smem], [gmem], 16; ``` This is available from Ampere and works on Hopper. It initiates async copy from global to shared, bypassing L1 and registers. I can use this to preload the next K/V tile while computing the current one. This is double buffering and hides memory latency. Let's design the kernel with pipelining: ```cuda __global__ void flash_attn_kernel(...) { // shared memory for double buffering // Q is loaded once. // K and V have double buffers. // Stage 0: Initiate async load of K[0], V[0] // For iter j=0 to num_kv_tiles-1: // wait on K[j], V[j] // compute Q @ Kj^T // softmax // compute Pj @ Vj -> O // if j+1 < num_kv_tiles: async load K[j+1], V[j+1] } ``` This is a standard software pipelining approach. We can vectorize. SMEM load optimization: - Q is stored as [Br][d] row-major. Each row is 128*2=256 bytes. - Thread reads Q[row][d_idx]. For 4 rows, 4 loads. - K is stored as [Bc][d] row-major. For 16 cols, 16 loads. - To avoid bank conflicts, threads in a warp should access different banks. Let's check Q access: row_group = tid / 8. So threads 0..7 are row_group 0. Threads 8..15 are row_group 1, etc. Wait, tid 0: row_group=0, tid 1: row_group=0, ..., tid 7: row_group=0. tid 8: row_group=1, etc. So 8 threads per row_group. These 8 threads access rows 0..3? No, rows 0..3 for row_group 0. Wait, with 8 threads on the same 4 rows, each accessing different cols of Q? Actually for QK, each thread needs the same Q rows but different K columns. So threads in a warp have the same row_group but different col_thread. For Q access: threads in a warp (32 threads) might span 4 row groups (since 8 threads per row_group). Warp 0: tids 0..31 -> row_groups 0..3 (4 groups). Wait, 32 threads / 8 = 4 row groups. Each row group uses 4 rows. So warp 0 accesses rows 0..15. These are 16 different rows. Addresses in smem: row * 256 + d_idx * 2. If d_idx is the same for all threads (it is, we loop d_idx), then addresses differ by multiples of 256 bytes. 256 bytes = 64 banks (since 4 bytes/bank). Actually 256 / 4 = 64. 64 % 32 = 0. So all threads would hit the same bank for different rows? Bank = (address / 4) % 32 = (row * 64 + d_idx/2) % 32. Row 0: bank = d_idx/2 % 32 (since row * 64 mod 32 = 0). Row 1: bank = (64 + d_idx/2) % 32 = d_idx/2 % 32. Same bank! Row 2: 128 mod 32 = 0. Same bank! This is TERRIBLE! Every thread in a warp accessing the same column but different rows will hit the same bank. To fix this: we need to pad the row dimension or transpose Q in shared memory. If Q_smem pitch is (d + pad) halfs. Pad such that consecutive rows hit different banks. Since row pitch = (d+pad)*2 bytes. We want pitch/4 % 32 != 0. (d+pad)*2/4 = (d+pad)/2. We want (128+pad)/2 % 32 != 0. If pad=0: 64 % 32 = 0. Bad. If pad=2 halfs: 130/2 = 65. 65 % 32 = 1. Good! So pitch = 130 halfs = 260 bytes. Then row i address = i*260 + d_idx*2. Bank = (i*65 + d_idx/2) % 32. For consecutive rows, bank shifts by 65 % 32 = 1. So each row is offset by 1 bank. No conflicts for threads accessing same column in consecutive rows. So Q_smem pitch should be 130 bf16 = 260 bytes. Similarly for K_smem and V_smem. For K access: K is [Bc][d] row-major. Each thread accesses a different key column (our 16 unique cols per thread). But multiple threads in a warp might access overlapping cols? Actually col_thread = tid % 8. So threads 0,8,16,24 are col_thread=0. They access K columns [0,16,..] relative to their col_thread*16 + subcol. Wait, within a warp of 32 threads: tid 0: col_thread=0, accesses K cols 0..15 tid 1: col_thread=1, accesses K cols 16..31 ... tid 7: col_thread=7, accesses K cols 112..127 tid 8: col_thread=0, row_group=1, accesses K cols 0..15 again. So in a warp of 32 threads, 4 threads have the same col_thread (0,8,16,24 all col_thread=0). They all access the same 16 columns of K! So they'll read the same values or different values depending on subcol iteration. If we iterate cc=0..15 and all 4 threads read K[0][d_idx] simultaneously, that's a broadcast (single address, no conflict). Wait, K[cc][d_idx] where cc is the column in K, same for all 4 threads. Same address. Broadcast is handled efficiently on GPUs (single read, broadcast to threads). Similarly for cc=1, all 4 threads read K[1][d_idx], another broadcast. So K access has no bank conflicts because same-address reads are broadcast. Q access has bank conflicts without padding, but fixed with pitch=130. Similarly for V: the PV product accesses V[k][d] where k is the key index and d is our assigned output column. For the PV outer product, we'll loop over k and read V[k][d_col]. Different threads in a warp have different d_col (they handle different output cols). Since V is stored [Bc][d], reading V[k][d_col] for different d_col across threads: addresses differ by column offset. V[k][d_col] address = k*pitch + d_col*2. For fixed k, as d_col varies across threads, addresses are consecutive 2-byte offsets. With 32 threads, reading consecutive bf16... this causes 2-way bank conflicts (2 bytes per element, 4 bytes per bank). Solution: Store V in transpose [d][Bc] or use pitch such that d_col offset spreads across banks. If V is transposed to [d][Bc] with pitch Bc_padded: Address = d_col * Bc_pitch + k * 2. For fixed k, as d_col varies, addresses differ by Bc_pitch bytes. If Bc_pitch = 132 bytes (66 halfs), then consecutive d_col increases address by 132 bytes. Bank increases by 132/4 = 33 % 32 = 1. No conflicts! So we should store V transposed as V_T[d][Bc_padded] in shared memory. We load V from global memory and transpose during the async copy. This is standard in optimized matmul kernels. For inline CUDA, I can manually do the transpose during the copy to shared memory. But wait, for simplicity and time, maybe I can just accept some bank conflicts and rely on H100's performance? H100 has much higher shared memory bandwidth than A100. Actually, let's check H100 shared memory bandwidth. H100 shared memory throughput is very high, close to 10-15 TB/s per SM. A few bank conflicts won't kill us. Given the complexity of implementing perfect bank conflicts handling, I'll: 1. Pad Q and K rows by 2 halfs to avoid the major Q conflict. 2. For V, I'll process the PV product by iterating k and broadcasting V[k][d] or similar. Wait, for the PV outer product: Loop k = 0 to Bc-1: p_val = S_softmax[r][k] for each query row r in thread For each d_col in thread's columns: O[r][d_col] += p_val * V[k][d_col] Here, for a fixed k, threads in the same row_group need the same p_val (since all threads in the row_group handle the same 4 query rows). Threads in different row_groups need different p_vals. Within a warp of 32 threads: 4 row_groups. So 4 different p_vals. Each thread needs p_val for its 4 rows. We can keep p_vals in registers per row. For V[k][d_col]: each thread accesses different d_col. If V is stored row-major [Bc][d]: Address = k*pitch + d_col*2. Consecutive d_col -> consecutive addresses -> 2-way bank conflict. If we store V transposed [d][Bc]: Address = d_col*pitch + k*2. Consecutive d_col (within warp, different threads have different d_col) -> addresses differ by pitch. With pitch=132 bytes -> bank diff by 33 % 32 = 1. No conflict. So transposing V in SMEM is very beneficial for the PV phase. I'll do that. Similarly, for K in QK^T phase, storing K row-major is fine because the conflicting accesses are broadcasts. For Q in QK^T phase, padding by 2 halfs is needed. OK, I'll write the kernel now. Full kernel design: ```cuda // Constants constexpr int Br = 128; constexpr int Bc = 128; constexpr int D = 128; constexpr int NUM_THREADS = 256; constexpr int Q_ROWS_PER_THREAD = 4; constexpr int O_COLS_PER_THREAD = 16; // D/8 = 16, since 256/32=8 thread groups for cols // Wait: 256 threads. Mapping: // row_group = tid / 8 (0..31), each group has 4 rows. 32*4=128 rows. // col_thread = tid % 8 (0..7), each handles 16 cols. 8*16=128 cols. // Yes, Q_ROWS_PER_THREAD = 4 (we handle 4 rows of Q). // O_COLS_PER_THREAD = 16. // Shared memory // sQ: 128 x 130 x 2 bytes = 33280 bytes (~33KB) // sK: 128 x 130 x 2 bytes = 33280 bytes (~33KB) // sV: 128 x 132 x 2 bytes transposed? Wait V needs to be [d][Bc_padded]. // Let's store V as [d][Bc_padded] where Bc_padded = 66 (132 bytes). // sV: 128 x 66 x 2 = 16896 bytes (~17KB). Wait, 128x132? // Actually d=128, Bc_padded = 66 elements = 132 bytes. // sV: 128 * 66 * 2 = 16896 bytes. But V from global is 128x128. // We can load V global [Bc][D] and write to sV_t [D][Bc_padded]. // sS: 128 x 128 x 4 = 65536 bytes (64KB) - optionally // Alternatively, compute S and store in registers, then use SMEM for reduction. // For online softmax, we need row-wise max and sum. // We can compute per-thread partial max, then warp reduction, then block reduction. Wait, but online softmax requires the max over the entire KV tile to compute the correct scaling. However, the standard online algorithm works with chunks: For chunk c: m_prev, l_prev Compute S_chunk m_new = max(m_prev, rowmax(S_chunk)) scale = exp(m_prev - m_new) l_new = l_prev * scale + rowsum(exp(S_chunk - m_new)) O_new = O_prev * scale + matmul(exp(S_chunk - m_new), V_chunk) This is correct as long as we process chunks sequentially along the key dimension! Actually shared memory arrays are typically aligned to the element size or vector size. If we declare as `__nv_bfloat16 sV_T[D][Bc_padded];`, the inner dimension is in elements. Access: &sV_T[d][k]. Address = (d * Bc_padded + k) * sizeof(bf16). If Bc_padded = 65 elements = 130 bytes. Pitch = 130 bytes (even). If Bc_padded = 67 elements = 134 bytes (even). Hmm, odd byte pitches are unusual because bf16 is 2 bytes, so element pitch is naturally even. But we need pitch_bytes odd. That means pitch_elements must be half-integer? That's not possible with bf16 arrays. Wait, we could use a char array and index manually. Or we can make Bc_padded odd in terms of bytes by using a different type? If we store as `__half2` or `float2`, we get 4 bytes per element. Then pitch in elements * 4 bytes. If pitch_elements = 33, pitch_bytes = 132 (even). delta = 4*132 % 32 = 528 % 32 = 16. gcd(16,32)=16 -> only 2 distinct banks. Bad. If pitch_elements = 34, pitch_bytes = 136. delta = 544 % 32 = 0. Bad. Actually with 4-byte elements, we need delta_bytes (difference in address between consecutive col_threads) to have gcd(delta,32)=4. Address for col_thread accessing first element: d_start * pitch_bytes. d_start difference = 16. delta_address = 16 * pitch_bytes. delta_bank = 16 * pitch_bytes / 4 % 32 = 4 * pitch_bytes % 32. With pitch_bytes even: pitch_bytes = 2m. delta_bank = 8m % 32. gcd(8m, 32) >= 8. So number of distinct banks <= 4. We can only support 4 threads without conflict. This is a fundamental issue with transposed layout when each thread accesses a distinct block of 16 columns. 8 threads accessing float4 from transposed memory cause conflicts because the stride is too large. Alternative: Don't transpose V. Keep V row-major in SMEM. Then threads access V[k][d_base + subcol]. For row-major V: address = k * pitch_bytes + d * 2. For fixed k, bank depends on d*2/4 = d/2. Threads have d_base differing by 16. d_base/2 differs by 8. Banks differ by 8. Same issue: delta=8 -> 4 distinct banks. Can we use double-buffered conflict-free access? Actually, bank conflicts reduce throughput but aren't fatal. 4-way or 2-way conflict means 4x or 2x SMEM cycles. SMEM bandwidth is ~10-20 TB/s per SM. Even 8-way conflict still gives ~1-2 TB/s which might be enough. For H100, shared memory bandwidth is approximately: - ~10-15 TB/s per SM in theory. With conflicts, maybe 2-5 TB/s effective. - We need: for PV, each thread does ~2048 loads of 2 bytes = 4KB over the tile. - 256 threads = 1MB SMEM traffic per block. - At 5 TB/s, that's 0.2 us. Negligible. Wait, 1MB per block at 5TB/s is indeed 0.2us. If we have 2 waves that's 0.4us. Very small overhead. So I will not obsess over perfect bank conflict freedom. I'll use reasonable padding (e.g., pitch=66 halfs = 132 bytes) and accept minor conflicts. OK, let me write the code now. Actually, I realize there's a much simpler shared memory layout trick for V access: Instead of transposing V, iterate k in the outer loop and have threads coalesce along d. When reading V for PV: For k = 0..Bc-1: // All threads read V[k] for their assigned d columns. // If V is row-major, and each thread reads 16 consecutive bf16 starting at col_thread*16, // This is a contiguous float4 x 2 access per thread. // Across 32 threads in a warp: 32 * 16 * 2 = 1024 bytes accessed. // Addresses: thread i: base + i*32. // Wait, if 32 threads are in 4 row_groups (8 col_threads are duplicated across 4 row_groups): // row_group 0 and row_group 1 both have col_threads 0..7. // In a warp of 32 threads (threads 0..31): row_groups 0..3. // col_threads 0..7 appear in each of 4 row_groups. // So 4 threads access col_start=0, 4 threads access col_start=16, ..., 4 threads access col_start=112. // For V[k][0]: threads 0,8,16,24 all read the same address. For PV, a thread needs V[k][d] for its specific output O columns. The O columns are the same regardless of which query row group. Yes! All threads with the same col_thread handle the same 16 output columns. So they all read the exact same V[k] slice. This is a perfect broadcast! 4 threads reading identical address: handled by broadcast (no bank conflict, just a single load broadcast to 4 threads). But wait, each thread also needs V[k] for different k as k iterates. No issue there. What about Q access for QK^T computation? Q access: each thread needs Q[r][d] for its 4 rows. Threads in the same row_group (8 threads) all need the same 4 rows! So they read the same Q values (broadcast). Threads in different row_groups (0..3 in a warp) read different rows. With Q padded appropriately, this can be made conflict-free. So V access is naturally broadcast-heavy and thus conflict-free if we keep V row-major and iterate k inside. This is great! So keep V row-major. K access for QK: each thread reads K for its 16 cols. Threads with same col_thread read same K columns. In a warp, 4 threads have same col_thread (in 4 different row_groups). Address = k_addr + (col_thread*16 + subcol)*2. For a given subcol, threads with same col_thread but different row_groups read the same address. Broadcast. No wait, different col_threads access DIFFERENT columns. Columns 0..127. For subcol=0: col_thread=c accesses column c*16. Address offset = c*16*2 = c*32 bytes. Bank offset = c*8. This means col_threads 0 and 4 both offset by 0 and 32 banks (0 mod 32). They conflict! But col_thread 0 and 4 are in the same warp. They read K columns 0 and 64. Actually, shared memory can handle multiple accesses to the same bank as long as they are to different addresses (multi-ported to some degree), but in practice 32-bit mode allows 2-way or depends on GPU generation. For Q: shape [batch, num_heads, seq, D]. Each row is contiguous in D. For a Q tile of 128 rows, we load Q[row][d] for row in tile and d=0..127. Each row is 256 bytes = 4 float4s. With 256 threads, loading 128*128 bf16 = 32768 elements = 16384 float2s = 8192 float4s. 8192 float4 loads / 256 threads = 32 float4 loads per thread. Nice and coalesced. For K and V: similar. Now let me sketch the entire kernel: ```cuda constexpr int D = 128; constexpr int Br = 128; constexpr int Bc = 128; constexpr int NUM_THREADS = 256; constexpr int NUM_WARPS = NUM_THREADS / 32; // 8 constexpr int ROWS_PER_THREAD = 4; // each thread handles 4 rows constexpr int COLS_PER_THREAD = 16; // each thread handles 16 cols of head_dim constexpr int Q_ROW_GROUPS = Br / ROWS_PER_THREAD; // 32 constexpr int O_COL_THREADS = D / COLS_PER_THREAD; // 8 // Shared memory pitch in elements (bf16 pitch) constexpr int Q_SMEM_PAD = 2; // pad rows to avoid conflicts constexpr int Q_SMEM_WIDTH = D + Q_SMEM_PAD; // 130 halfs constexpr int K_SMEM_WIDTH = D + Q_SMEM_PAD; // 130 halfs constexpr int V_SMEM_WIDTH = D + 2; // 130 halfs // Shared memory layout // sQ: [Br][Q_SMEM_WIDTH] bf16 // sK: [Bc][K_SMEM_WIDTH] bf16 // sV: [Bc][V_SMEM_WIDTH] bf16 template<typename T> __device__ __forceinline__ float to_float(T x) { return (float)x; } template<> __device__ __forceinline__ float to_float<__nv_bfloat16>(__nv_bfloat16 x) { return __bfloat162float(x); } // ... other helpers ... __global__ void flash_attn_kernel( const __nv_bfloat16* __restrict__ Q, const __nv_bfloat16* __restrict__ K, const __nv_bfloat16* __restrict__ V, __nv_bfloat16* __restrict__ O, int batch, int num_heads, int num_kv_heads, int seq_len, int head_dim, float scale ) { int tid = threadIdx.x; int q_tile = blockIdx.x; int head_id = blockIdx.y; int batch_idx = head_id / num_heads; int head_q = head_id % num_heads; int group_size = num_heads / num_kv_heads; int head_kv = head_q / group_size; int q_seq_start = q_tile * Br; int q_offset = ((batch_idx * num_heads + head_q) * seq_len + q_seq_start) * head_dim; int kv_offset = ((batch_idx * num_kv_heads + head_kv) * seq_len) * head_dim; extern __shared__ __nv_bfloat16 smem[]; __nv_bfloat16* sQ = smem; __nv_bfloat16* sK = sQ + Br * Q_SMEM_WIDTH; __nv_bfloat16* sV = sK + Bc * K_SMEM_WIDTH; // Load Q tile // Total: Br * D = 16384 elements. // Each thread loads 16384 / 256 = 64 elements = 32 float4. // Or: each row is 128 bf16 = 8 float4. 128 rows * 8 = 1024 float4. // 1024 / 256 threads = 4 float4 per thread. // Thread i loads row i/2, float4 i%2 + ... Let's make it simple. for (int i = tid; i < Br * D; i += NUM_THREADS) { int row = i / D; int col = i % D; int g_idx = q_offset + row * D + col; sQ[row * Q_SMEM_WIDTH + col] = (q_seq_start + row < seq_len) ? Q[g_idx] : __float2bfloat16(0.0f); } __syncthreads(); // Thread coordinates int row_group = tid / O_COL_THREADS; // 0..31, each handles 4 rows int col_thread = tid % O_COL_THREADS; // 0..7, each handles 16 cols int row_start = row_group * ROWS_PER_THREAD; // 0,4,8,...,124 int col_start = col_thread * COLS_PER_THREAD; // 0,16,32,...,112 // Persistent registers float O_reg[ROWS_PER_THREAD][COLS_PER_THREAD]; float m_reg[ROWS_PER_THREAD]; float l_reg[ROWS_PER_THREAD]; #pragma unroll for (int rr = 0; rr < ROWS_PER_THREAD; ++rr) { m_reg[rr] = -1e20f; // -inf l_reg[rr] = 0.0f; #pragma unroll for (int cc = 0; cc < COLS_PER_THREAD; ++cc) { O_reg[rr][cc] = 0.0f; } } // Number of KV tiles int num_kv_tiles = (seq_len + Bc - 1) / Bc; for (int kv_tile = 0; kv_tile < num_kv_tiles; ++kv_tile) { int kv_seq_start = kv_tile * Bc; // Load K and V tiles for (int i = tid; i < Bc * D; i += NUM_THREADS) { int row = i / D; int col = i % D; int k_idx = kv_offset + (kv_seq_start + row) * D + col; __nv_bfloat16 k_val = (kv_seq_start + row < seq_len) ? K[k_idx] : __float2bfloat16(0.0f); __nv_bfloat16 v_val = (kv_seq_start + row < seq_len) ? V[k_idx] : __float2bfloat16(0.0f); sK[row * K_SMEM_WIDTH + col] = k_val; sV[row * V_SMEM_WIDTH + col] = v_val; } __syncthreads(); // Compute S = Q @ K^T for this tile // S_reg[ROWS_PER_THREAD][COLS_PER_THREAD]? No, COLS_PER_THREAD here would be key cols. // Each thread handles 4 query rows and ALL 128 key columns? Too much. // Wait, for QK^T, the output is Br x Bc = 128 x 128. // We need to distribute this 128x128 across threads. // If each thread handled 4 query rows x 16 key cols = 64 elements of S. This is the same as O! // So let's redefine: for QK, the matrix is 128x128, and each thread computes a 4x16 tile of S. // But earlier I said col_thread handles 16 cols of head_dim. For S, column dimension is Bc=128. // So thread could handle 4 rows x 16 key columns. 32 row groups * 8 col_threads = 256. // 8 key col threads * 16 = 128 key cols. If I use the same thread mapping for QK (128x128) as for O (128x128), then S_reg needs 64 FP32. Plus O_reg 64 FP32. Plus other regs. That's 128+ just for accumulators. We need O_reg[rr][cc]. Hmm, we have a mapping conflict between QK cols (key dimension) and O cols (head_dim dimension). During QK, col_thread handles 4 key columns. During O/PV, col_thread handles 16 head_dim columns. These are different mappings! We can't have both simultaneously without complex data movement. This means if we chunk KV tile into 32-column pieces, the thread mapping for QK output (key cols) is different from the thread mapping for O (head dim cols). After computing P_reg for 4 key cols, we need to multiply with V (4 rows of V) to update our O columns (16 head_dim cols). The outer product: For each k_idx = key_start..key_start+31: We need p = P[query_row][k_idx] for all query rows. And v = V[k_idx][head_dim] for our head dim cols. We accumulate O[query_row][head_dim] += p * v. For this, the natural mapping is by output O. Each thread handles some query rows and some head_dim cols. It needs P for its query rows and ALL k_idx in the chunk. And V for ALL k_idx in the chunk and its head_dim cols. If thread handles 1 query row and 16 head dim cols (or 4 rows and 16 cols): - Needs P for 4 rows x 32 key cols = 128 values per chunk. - Needs V for 32 key cols x 16 head dim cols per query row? Actually V is shared across rows. V needed: 32 x 16 = 512 bf16 values = 1KB. This can be loaded from SMEM per chunk. But P is the issue. Where does P come from? We compute S chunk. Each thread handles different query rows and key cols. To get P for a specific k_idx and a thread's query rows, we either: 1. Store P chunk in SMEM (128 x 32). 128*32*4 = 16KB. Each thread then reads P for its 4 rows and all 32 k_idx (128 float4 reads). This is feasible! 2. Or recompute S on demand for PV? No. So the approach: - Compute S_chunk (128x32) distributed in threads. - Use warp/block shuffle to compute row max and row sum, update m, l. - Write P_chunk (exp(S-m_new)) to a SMEM buffer of size 128x32 FP32 or BF16. - Then compute PV using the SMEM P and SMEM V. Each thread loads P for its 4 rows x 32 key cols from SMEM. And loads V for 32 key cols x 16 head dim cols. Outer product: 4 rows x 16 cols x 32 dot products = 2048 FMAs per chunk. Register usage during PV: - O_reg: 4*16 = 64 FP32 - P_buf: 4*32 = 128 FP32. Too many! Solution: process k_idx one by one. For k_idx = 0..31: Load p_vec[4] for this k_idx from SMEM. Load v_vec[16] for this k_idx from SMEM. O_reg[rr][cc] += p_vec[rr] * v_vec[cc] This only needs 4 + 16 = 20 additional registers. Brilliant! So P only needs to be in SMEM, not registers. We read it one key at a time. Total algorithm: For kv_tile: Load K, V to SMEM. For chunk=0..3 // 32 keys per chunk // Compute S_chunk into SMEM Each thread computes 4x4 tile of S_chunk (since 128*32/256=16 elements per thread: 4 rows x 4 key cols). Actually with 256 threads: 32 row_groups of 4 rows * 8 col_groups of 4 cols. S_reg[4][4] = 16 FP32. Good. After d-loop, write S_reg to sS_chunk (128x32 FP32 or BF16). __syncthreads(); // Softmax on chunk Each thread computes local max for its 4 query rows over its 4 key cols. (max over S_reg). Warp shuffle/block reduce to get row max over 32 key cols per query row. Update m_reg. Compute P_reg = exp(S_reg - m_new). Compute local sum = sum(P_reg). Warp shuffle/block reduce to get row sum. Update l_reg. Write P_reg to sP_chunk (128 x 32 BF16 or FP32). __syncthreads(); // PV contribution For k_local = 0..31: // or k_idx in chunk // Each thread loads p for its 4 rows at this k_idx float p_vals[4]; #pragma unroll for (int rr=0; rr<4; ++rr) p_vals[rr] = sP[(row_start+rr)*32 + k_local]; // Load v for its 16 head_dim cols __nv_bfloat16 v_vals[COLS_PER_THREAD]; #pragma unroll for (int cc=0; cc<COLS_PER_THREAD; ++cc) v_vals[cc] = sV[(k_global)*V_SMEM_WIDTH + (col_start+cc)]; #pragma unroll for (int rr=0; rr<4; ++rr) for (int cc=0; cc<COLS_PER_THREAD; ++cc) O_reg[rr][cc] += p_vals[rr] * to_float(v_vals[cc]); __syncthreads(); Wait, we also need to rescale O_reg by exp(m_old - m_new) before adding the new PV contribution! In FlashAttention: scale = exp(m_old - m_new) O_reg *= scale O_reg += P_chunk @ V_chunk l_reg = l_old * scale + rowsum(P) So after computing row max and before PV, we need to scale O_reg. We can do this right after computing the new m and before writing P to SMEM. Sequence within chunk: 1. Compute S_reg. 2. Find row max of S_chunk (block reduction). 3. Compute m_new, scale = exp(m_old - m_new). 4. O_reg *= scale. (each thread scales its O_reg) 5. l_reg *= scale. 6. Compute P_reg = exp(S_reg - m_new). 7. l_reg += rowsum(P_reg) (block reduction). 8. Write P_reg to SMEM. 9. Compute PV contribution and add to O_reg. 10. m_old = m_new. This matches the FlashAttention algorithm exactly! And we never need to store the full 128x128 S or P matrices. Only one 128x32 chunk (16KB for FP32 or 8KB for BF16) in SMEM at a time. SMEM budget: - sQ: 128 * 132 * 2 = 33792 bytes (~33KB) - sK: 128 * 132 * 2 = 33792 bytes (~33KB) - sV: 128 * 132 * 2 = 33792 bytes (~33KB) - sP_chunk: 128 * 32 * 2 = 8192 bytes (BF16) or 16384 (FP32). Let's use FP32 (16KB) for better accuracy. Total: ~97KB. Great! We have room to grow if needed. Wait, but sK and sV both store the full 128x128 KV tile. That's 33KB each = 66KB. Q is 33KB. Actually wait, the grid is (seq_len/Br=16, batch*num_heads=128) = 2048. With 2 blocks per SM concurrently, we have 264 concurrent blocks. 2048/264 = 7.75. Nice! I think this design is solid and definitely implementable. Now let me write the complete code. One remaining issue: numerical precision. - We need to scale by sqrt(D) = sqrt(128) ≈ 11.3137085. - In FlashAttention, scale is typically applied before the QK matmul or during. - We'll apply during QK: S = dot(Q, K) * scale. For BF16/fp16 attention, some implementations do the matmul in FP32 with the scale included. We'll do the same for matching numerics. Also, the hidden reference might or might not include casual masking. The original code had `false` for causal (likely). And the I/O contract doesn't mention causal, so non-causal. Let me draft the complete CUDA kernel. ```cuda #include <cuda_bf16.h> #include <cuda_runtime.h> #include <math.h> #define D 128 #define Br 128 #define Bc 128 #define NUM_THREADS 256 #define ROWS_PER_THREAD 4 #define COLS_PER_THREAD 16 #define Q_ROW_GROUPS (Br / ROWS_PER_THREAD) // 32 #define O_COL_THREADS (D / COLS_PER_THREAD) // 8 #define CHUNK_K 32 #define NUM_CHUNKS (Bc / CHUNK_K) // 4 #define Q_PITCH (D + 2) #define K_PITCH (D + 2) #define V_PITCH (D + 2) __device__ __forceinline__ float to_float(__nv_bfloat16 x) { return __bfloat162float(x); } __device__ __forceinline__ __nv_bfloat16 to_bf16(float x) { return __float2bfloat16(x); } // Block reduction for max across a warp __device__ __forceinline__ float warp_reduce_max(float val) { #pragma unroll for (int offset = 16; offset > 0; offset /= 2) val = fmaxf(val, __shfl_xor_sync(0xffffffff, val, offset)); return val; } // Block reduction for sum across a warp __device__ __forceinline__ float warp_reduce_sum(float val) { #pragma unroll for (int offset = 16; offset > 0; offset /= 2) val += __shfl_xor_sync(0xffffffff, val, offset); return val; } __global__ void flash_attn_fwd_bf16( const __nv_bfloat16* __restrict__ Q, const __nv_bfloat16* __restrict__ K, const __nv_bfloat16* __restrict__ V, __nv_bfloat16* __restrict__ O, int batch, int num_heads, int num_kv_heads, int seq_len, float scale ) { int tid = threadIdx.x; int q_tile = blockIdx.x; int head_id = blockIdx.y; int batch_idx = head_id / num_heads; int head_q = head_id % num_heads; int group_size = num_heads / num_kv_heads; int head_kv = head_q / group_size; int q_seq_start = q_tile * Br; int q_offset = ((batch_idx * num_heads + head_q) * seq_len + q_seq_start) * D; int kv_offset = ((batch_idx * num_kv_heads + head_kv) * seq_len) * D; extern __shared__ __nv_bfloat16 smem[]; __nv_bfloat16* sQ = smem; __nv_bfloat16* sK = sQ + Br * Q_PITCH; __nv_bfloat16* sV = sK + Bc * K_PITCH; float* sP = (float*)(sV + Bc * V_PITCH); // FP32 scratch for P_chunk // Load Q tile: coalesced float4 loads // Each thread loads Br*D / NUM_THREADS = 64 elements = 32 float2 = 16 float4 per thread int q_elems = Br * D; for (int idx = tid; idx < q_elems; idx += NUM_THREADS) { int row = idx / D; int col = idx % D; int g_idx = q_offset + row * D + col; __nv_bfloat16 val = (q_seq_start + row < seq_len) ? Q[g_idx] : to_bf16(0.0f); sQ[row * Q_PITCH + col] = val; } __syncthreads(); int row_group = tid / O_COL_THREADS; // 0..31 int col_thread = tid % O_COL_THREADS; // 0..7 int row_start = row_group * ROWS_PER_THREAD; int col_start = col_thread * COLS_PER_THREAD; float O_reg[ROWS_PER_THREAD][COLS_PER_THREAD]; float m_reg[ROWS_PER_THREAD]; float l_reg[ROWS_PER_THREAD]; float S_reg[ROWS_PER_THREAD][CHUNK_K / O_COL_THREADS]; // 4 x 4 = 16 #pragma unroll for (int rr = 0; rr < ROWS_PER_THREAD; ++rr) { m_reg[rr] = -1e30f; l_reg[rr] = 0.0f; #pragma unroll for (int cc = 0; cc < COLS_PER_THREAD; ++cc) { O_reg[rr][cc] = 0.0f; } } int num_kv_tiles = (seq_len + Bc - 1) / Bc; for (int kv_tile = 0; kv_tile < num_kv_tiles; ++kv_tile) { int kv_seq_start = kv_tile * Bc; // Async/coalesced load of K and V tiles int kv_elems = Bc * D; for (int idx = tid; idx < kv_elems; idx += NUM_THREADS) { int row = idx / D; int col = idx % D; int k_idx = kv_offset + (kv_seq_start + row) * D + col; bool valid = kv_seq_start + row < seq_len; sK[row * K_PITCH + col] = valid ? K[k_idx] : to_bf16(0.0f); sV[row * V_PITCH + col] = valid ? V[k_idx] : to_bf16(0.0f); } __syncthreads(); // Process KV tile in 4 chunks of 32 keys #pragma unroll for (int chunk = 0; chunk < NUM_CHUNKS; ++chunk) { int k_chunk_start = chunk * CHUNK_K; int cols_per_warp = CHUNK_K / O_COL_THREADS; // 4 // Zero S_reg #pragma unroll for (int rr = 0; rr < ROWS_PER_THREAD; ++rr) { #pragma unroll for (int cc = 0; cc < cols_per_warp; ++cc) { S_reg[rr][cc] = 0.0f; } } // Compute Q @ K^T chunk: S[Br][CHUNK_K] // Each thread: 4 query rows x 4 key cols #pragma unroll for (int d = 0; d < D; ++d) { // Load Q for 4 rows float q_vals[ROWS_PER_THREAD]; #pragma unroll for (int rr = 0; rr < ROWS_PER_THREAD; ++rr) { q_vals[rr] = to_float(sQ[(row_start + rr) * Q_PITCH + d]); } // Load K for 4 key cols float k_vals[4]; #pragma unroll for (int cc = 0; cc < cols_per_warp; ++cc) { int k_col = k_chunk_start + col_thread * cols_per_warp + cc; k_vals[cc] = to_float(sK[k_col * K_PITCH + d]); } // Outer product accumulate #pragma unroll for (int rr = 0; rr < ROWS_PER_THREAD; ++rr) { #pragma unroll for (int cc = 0; cc < cols_per_warp; ++cc) { S_reg[rr][cc] += q_vals[rr] * k_vals[cc]; } } } // Apply scale #pragma unroll for (int rr = 0; rr < ROWS_PER_THREAD; ++rr) { #pragma unroll for (int cc = 0; cc < cols_per_warp; ++cc) { S_reg[rr][cc] *= scale; } } // Row max reduction per row within warp/block float row_max[ROWS_PER_THREAD]; #pragma unroll for (int rr = 0; rr < ROWS_PER_THREAD; ++rr) { float local_max = S_reg[rr][0]; #pragma unroll for (int cc = 1; cc < cols_per_warp; ++cc) { local_max = fmaxf(local_max, S_reg[rr][cc]); } // Reduce with other threads in the row group (same row_group, different col_thread) // row_group has 8 consecutive threads (col_thread 0..7). // They are all in the same warp since 32 threads per warp contain 4 row_groups. // Actually warp 0: row_groups 0,1,2,3. Each has 8 threads. Total 32. // So row_group is within a warp! Yes: threads in a row_group are consecutive. // col_thread 0..7 are tid%8. // Warp reduction works across all 32 threads, but we only want max over col_threads for same row_group. // We can use shfl with lane mask or just reduce within the warp and have 4 identical values per row_group. // Since 4 row_groups in a warp, warp_red_max gives max over all 4 row_groups and all col_threads. // But we want per-row max. // Each row is only processed by col_threads 0..7 in the same row_group. // Other row_groups in the warp process different rows. // We need row-wise max across the 8 col_threads for THIS row_group only. // Use a sub-warp shfl within the row_group. // Row_group threads are in lanes: row_group*8 + 0..7. // We can use __shfl_xor_sync with a mask covering just the row_group? // Actually shfl operates within the warp. As long as lane_id differs, it reads the value. // We can max over the 8 threads: #pragma unroll for (int offset = 4; offset > 0; offset /= 2) { local_max = fmaxf(local_max, __shfl_xor_sync(0xffffffff, local_max, offset)); } row_max[rr] = local_max; } // Update m and scale O, l float m_prev[ROWS_PER_THREAD]; #pragma unroll for (int rr = 0; rr < ROWS_PER_THREAD; ++rr) { m_prev[rr] = m_reg[rr]; m_reg[rr] = fmaxf(m_reg[rr], row_max[rr]); float scale_factor = expf(m_prev[rr] - m_reg[rr]); #pragma unroll for (int cc = 0; cc < COLS_PER_THREAD; ++cc) { O_reg[rr][cc] *= scale_factor; } l_reg[rr] *= scale_factor; } // Compute P = exp(S - m_new) and local sum float row_sum[ROWS_PER_THREAD]; #pragma unroll for (int rr = 0; rr < ROWS_PER_THREAD; ++rr) { row_sum[rr] = 0.0f; #pragma unroll for (int cc = 0; cc < cols_per_warp; ++cc) { S_reg[rr][cc] = expf(S_reg[rr][cc] - m_reg[rr]); row_sum[rr] += S_reg[rr][cc]; } } // Reduce row_sum across col_threads #pragma unroll for (int rr = 0; rr < ROWS_PER_THREAD; ++rr) { float local_sum = row_sum[rr]; #pragma unroll for (int offset = 4; offset > 0; offset /= 2) { local_sum += __shfl_xor_sync(0xffffffff, local_sum, offset); } l_reg[rr] += local_sum; } // Write P to SMEM (128 x 32 float scratch) // Each thread writes 4x4 = 16 floats #pragma unroll for (int rr = 0; rr < ROWS_PER_THREAD; ++rr) { #pragma unroll for (int cc = 0; cc < cols_per_warp; ++cc) { int k_col = k_chunk_start + col_thread * cols_per_warp + cc; sP[(row_start + rr) * CHUNK_K + k_col] = S_reg[rr][cc]; } } __syncthreads(); // Compute PV contribution: O += P_chunk @ V_chunk // For each key in this chunk #pragma unroll for (int k = 0; k < CHUNK_K; ++k) { int global_k = k_chunk_start + k; float p_vals[ROWS_PER_THREAD]; #pragma unroll for (int rr = 0; rr < ROWS_PER_THREAD; ++rr) { p_vals[rr] = sP[(row_start + rr) * CHUNK_K + k]; } // Load V for this key and our head_dim columns float v_vals[COLS_PER_THREAD]; #pragma unroll for (int cc = 0; cc < COLS_PER_THREAD; ++cc) { v_vals[cc] = to_float(sV[global_k * V_PITCH + (col_start + cc)]); } #pragma unroll for (int rr = 0; rr < ROWS_PER_THREAD; ++rr) { #pragma unroll for (int cc = 0; cc < COLS_PER_THREAD; ++cc) { O_reg[rr][cc] += p_vals[rr] * v_vals[cc]; } } } __syncthreads(); } } // Finalize: divide by l #pragma unroll for (int rr = 0; rr < ROWS_PER_THREAD; ++rr) { float inv_l = 1.0f / l_reg[rr]; #pragma unroll for (int cc = 0; cc < COLS_PER_THREAD; ++cc) { O_reg[rr][cc] *= inv_l; } } // Write O to global memory #pragma unroll for (int rr = 0; rr < ROWS_PER_THREAD; ++rr) { int row = q_seq_start + row_start + rr; if (row < seq_len) { int o_offset = ((batch_idx * num_heads + head_q) * seq_len + row) * D; #pragma unroll for (int cc = 0; cc < COLS_PER_THREAD; cc += 8) { // Store 8 bf16 at a time using two uint2 or float4 // Actually float4 is 16 bytes = 8 bf16. // Let's do simple store for now. // Or use __nv_bfloat162 for pairs. } } } } ``` Wait, I need to finish the store. assistant```python import torch import torch.nn as nn import os os.environ['TORCH_CUDA_ARCH_LIST'] = '9.0' CUDA_SOURCE = r''' #include <cuda_bf16.h> #include <cuda_runtime.h> #include <math.h> #define D 128 #define Br 128 #define Bc 128 #define NUM_THREADS 256 #define ROWS_PER_THREAD 4 #define COLS_PER_THREAD 16 #define Q_ROW_GROUPS (Br / ROWS_PER_THREAD) // 32 #define O_COL_THREADS (D / COLS_PER_THREAD) // 8 #define CHUNK_K 32 #define NUM_CHUNKS (Bc / CHUNK_K) // 4 #define Q_PITCH 130 #define K_PITCH 130 #define V_PITCH 130 __device__ __forceinline__ float to_float(__nv_bfloat16 x) { return __bfloat162float(x); } __device__ __forceinline__ __nv_bfloat16 to_bf16(float x) { return __float2bfloat16_rn(x); } __device__ __forceinline__ float warp_reduce_max(float val) { #pragma unroll for (int offset = 4; offset > 0; offset >>= 1) val = fmaxf(val, __shfl_xor_sync(0xffffffff, val, offset)); return val; } __device__ __forceinline__ float warp_reduce_sum(float val) { #pragma unroll for (int offset = 4; offset > 0; offset >>= 1) val += __shfl_xor_sync(0xffffffff, val, offset); return val; } __global__ void __launch_bounds__(NUM_THREADS, 1) flash_attn_fwd_bf16( const __nv_bfloat16* __restrict__ Q, const __nv_bfloat16* __restrict__ K, const __nv_bfloat16* __restrict__ V, __nv_bfloat16* __restrict__ O, int batch, int num_heads, int num_kv_heads, int seq_len, float scale ) { int tid = threadIdx.x; int q_tile = blockIdx.x; int head_id = blockIdx.y; int batch_idx = head_id / num_heads; int head_q = head_id % num_heads; int group_size = num_heads / num_kv_heads; int head_kv = head_q / group_size; int q_seq_start = q_tile * Br; if (q_seq_start >= seq_len) return; int q_offset = ((batch_idx * num_heads + head_q) * seq_len + q_seq_start) * D; int kv_offset = ((batch_idx * num_kv_heads + head_kv) * seq_len) * D; extern __shared__ __nv_bfloat16 smem[]; __nv_bfloat16* sQ = smem; __nv_bfloat16* sK = sQ + Br * Q_PITCH; __nv_bfloat16* sV = sK + Bc * K_PITCH; float* sP = (float*)(sV + Bc * V_PITCH); // Load Q tile using __nv_bfloat162 vector loads int q_vec_elems = Br * D / 2; sQ[row * Q_PITCH + col2 * 2 + 1] = val.y; } __syncthreads(); int row_group = tid / O_COL_THREADS; // 0..31 int col_thread = tid % O_COL_THREADS; // 0..7 int row_start = row_group * ROWS_PER_THREAD; // 0,4,8,...,124 int col_start = col_thread * COLS_PER_THREAD; // 0,16,32,...,112 constexpr int cols_per_warp = CHUNK_K / O_COL_THREADS; // 4 float O_reg[ROWS_PER_THREAD][COLS_PER_THREAD]; float m_reg[ROWS_PER_THREAD]; float l_reg[ROWS_PER_THREAD]; float S_reg[ROWS_PER_THREAD][cols_per_warp]; #pragma unroll for (int rr = 0; rr < ROWS_PER_THREAD; ++rr) { m_reg[rr] = -1e30f; l_reg[rr] = 0.0f; #pragma unroll for (int cc = 0; cc < COLS_PER_THREAD; ++cc) { O_reg[rr][cc] = 0.0f; } } int num_kv_tiles = (seq_len + Bc - 1) / Bc; for (int kv_tile = 0; kv_tile < num_kv_tiles; ++kv_tile) { int kv_seq_start = kv_tile * Bc; // Load K and V tiles int kv_vec_elems = Bc * D / 2; for (int i = tid; i < kv_vec_elems; i += NUM_THREADS) { int row = i / (D / 2); int col2 = i % (D / 2); int k_idx = kv_offset + (kv_seq_start + row) * D + col2 * 2; bool valid = (kv_seq_start + row) < seq_len; __nv_bfloat162 kval = valid ? reinterpret_cast<const __nv_bfloat162*>(K)[k_idx / 2] : make_bfloat162(0.0f, 0.0f); __nv_bfloat162 vval = valid ? reinterpret_cast<const __nv_bfloat162*>(V)[k_idx / 2] : make_bfloat162(0.0f, 0.0f); sK[row * K_PITCH + col2 * 2] = kval.x; sK[row * K_PITCH + col2 * 2 + 1] = kval.y; sV[row * V_PITCH + col2 * 2] = vval.x; sV[row * V_PITCH + col2 * 2 + 1] = vval.y; } __syncthreads(); #pragma unroll for (int chunk = 0; chunk < NUM_CHUNKS; ++chunk) { int k_chunk_start = chunk * CHUNK_K; if (kv_seq_start + k_chunk_start >= seq_len) continue; // Zero accumulators for this chunk #pragma unroll for (int rr = 0; rr < ROWS_PER_THREAD; ++rr) { #pragma unroll for (int cc = 0; cc < cols_per_warp; ++cc) { S_reg[rr][cc] = 0.0f; } } // Compute S = Q @ K^T for this chunk #pragma unroll for (int d = 0; d < D; ++d) { float q_vals[ROWS_PER_THREAD]; #pragma unroll for (int rr = 0; rr < ROWS_PER_THREAD; ++rr) { q_vals[rr] = to_float(sQ[(row_start + rr) * Q_PITCH + d]); } float k_vals[cols_per_warp]; #pragma unroll for (int cc = 0; cc < cols_per_warp; ++cc) { S_reg[rr][cc] += q_vals[rr] * k_vals[cc]; } } } // Apply attention scale #pragma unroll for (int rr = 0; rr < ROWS_PER_THREAD; ++rr) { #pragma unroll for (int cc = 0; cc < cols_per_warp; ++cc) { S_reg[rr][cc] *= scale; } } // Masking for padding keys #pragma unroll for (int rr = 0; rr < ROWS_PER_THREAD; ++rr) { #pragma unroll for (int cc = 0; cc < cols_per_warp; ++cc) { int global_k = kv_seq_start + k_chunk_start + col_thread * cols_per_warp + cc; ++rr) { float local_max = S_reg[rr][0]; #pragma unroll for (int cc = 1; cc < cols_per_warp; ++cc) { local_max = fmaxf(local_max, S_reg[rr][cc]); } row_max[rr] = warp_reduce_max(local_max); } // Update running max/scale O and l #pragma unroll for (int rr = 0; rr < ROWS_PER_THREAD; ++rr) { float m_prev = m_reg[rr]; m_reg[rr] = fmaxf(m_prev, row_max[rr]); float scale_factor = expf(m_prev - m_reg[rr]); #pragma unroll for (int cc = 0; cc < COLS_PER_THREAD; ++cc) { O_reg[rr][cc] *= scale_factor; } l_reg[rr] *= scale_factor; } // Compute P = exp(S - m_new) #pragma unroll for (int rr = 0; rr < ROWS_PER_THREAD; ++rr) { #pragma unroll for (int cc = 0; cc < cols_per_warp; ++cc) { S_reg[rr][cc] = expf(S_reg[rr][cc] - m_reg[rr]); } } // Row sum reduction #pragma unroll for (int rr = 0; rr < ROWS_PER_THREAD; ++rr) { float local_sum = 0.0f; #pragma unroll for (int cc = 0; cc < cols_per_warp; ++cc) { local_sum += S_reg[rr][cc]; } l_reg[rr] += warp_reduce_sum(local_sum); } // Write P chunk to SMEM scratchpad #pragma unroll for (int rr = 0; rr < ROWS_PER_THREAD; ++rr) { #pragma unroll for (int cc = 0; cc < cols_per_warp; ++cc) { int k_col = col_thread * cols_per_warp + cc; sP[(row_start + rr) * CHUNK_K + k_col] = S_reg[rr][cc]; } } __syncthreads(); // PV accumulation: O += P_chunk @ V_chunk #pragma unroll for (int k = 0; k < CHUNK_K; ++k) { int global_k = k_chunk_start + k; float p_vals[ROWS_PER_THREAD]; #pragma unroll for (int rr = 0; rr < ROWS_PER_THREAD; ++rr) { p_vals[rr] = sP[(row_start + rr) * CHUNK_K + k]; } float v_vals[COLS_PER_THREAD]; #pragma unroll for (int cc = 0; cc < COLS_PER_THREAD; ++cc) { v_vals[cc] = to_float(sV[global_k * V_PITCH + (col_start + cc)]); } #pragma unroll for (int rr = 0; rr < ROWS_PER_THREAD; ++rr) { #pragma unroll for (int cc = 0; cc < COLS_PER_THREAD; ++cc) { O_reg[rr][cc] += p_vals[rr] * v_vals[cc]; } } } __syncthreads(); } } // Final normalization #pragma unroll for (int rr = 0; rr < ROWS_PER_THREAD; ++rr) { float inv_l = (l_reg[rr] > 0.0f) ? (1.0f / l_reg[rr]) : 0.0f; #pragma unroll for (int cc = 0; cc < COLS_PER_THREAD; ++cc) { O_reg[rr][cc] *= inv_l; } } // Write O to global memory using __nv_bfloat162 stores #pragma unroll for (int rr = 0; rr < ROWS_PER_THREAD; ++rr) { int row = q_seq_start + row_start + rr; if (row < seq_len) { int o_row_offset = ((batch_idx * num_heads + head_q) * seq_len + row) * D + col_start; #pragma unroll for (int cc = 0; cc < COLS_PER_THREAD; cc += 2) { __nv_bfloat162 out; out.x = to_bf16(O_reg[rr][cc]); out.y = to_bf16(O_reg[rr][cc + 1]); reinterpret_cast<__nv_bfloat16*>(O)[o_row_offset + cc] = out.x; reinterpret_cast<__nv_bfloat16*>(O)[o_row_offset + cc + 1] = out.y; } } } } ''' class ModelNew(nn.Module): def __init__(self, num_heads, num_kv_heads, head_dim): super().__init__() self.num_heads = num_heads self.num_kv_heads = num_kv_heads self.head_dim = head_dim # compile inline CUDA for Hopper (sm_90) self.cuda_module = torch.utils.cpp_extension.load_inline( name='flash_attn_hopper_128', cpp_sources='', cuda_sources=CUDA_SOURCE, extra_cuda_cflags=[ '-O3', '-gencode', 'arch=compute_90,code=sm_90', '-std=c++17', '-D__CUDA_NO_HALF_OPERATORS__', '-D__CUDA_NO_HALF_CONVERSIONS__', '-D__CUDA_NO_BFLOAT16_CONVERSIONS__' ], verbose=False, ) def forward(self, q, k, v): batch, num_heads, seq_len, head_dim = q.shape o = torch.empty_like(q) scale = 1.0 / (head_dim ** 0.5) # grid: tiles over sequence dimension x all query heads grid = ((seq_len + Br - 1) // Br, batch * num_heads) block = NUM_THREADS # shared memory size: # sQ: Br * 130 * 2 = 33280 # sK: Bc * 130 * 2 = 33280 # sV: Bc * 130 * 2 = 33280 # sP: Br * 32 * 4 = 16384 smem = 116224 self.cuda_module.flash_attn_fwd_bf16[grid, block, 0, smem]( q, k, v, o, batch, num_heads, self.num_kv_heads, seq_len, scale ) return o ```

If we recompute: for each Td chunk, recompute QK to get P, then do PV for that chunk. But that's doubling work. If we store P: Bc=64, Br=128, so P is 128x64 = 8K floats = 32KB. That's exactly our smem budget! Wait, if we process in Td chunks and recompute: For each KV tile: - Compute QK (scores) - Do softmax - For each Td chunk: - Load V tile cols - PV accumulate - Store O chunk to global memory? No, still need rescaling across tiles. Hmm, actually if we process in column chunks, we still need to maintain running O across tiles for each chunk. But we can do it chunk by chunk: Outer loop over Td chunks (4 chunks of 32 cols each): o_chunk[32] = 0, m = -inf, l = 0 For each KV tile: Compute QK -> P For j=0..Bc-1: For dd=0..31: o_chunk[dd] += P[j] * V[j][d0+dd] Normalize and write O[:, d0:d0+32] This recomputes QK 4 times, but QK is relatively cheap compared to PV for the full D. Actually, QK is O(N*D) and PV is O(N*D) too, so recomputing QK 4 times adds 3x overhead. Not good. What if we store P in registers? For 1 thread handling 1 row, P for Bc=64 keys is 64 floats. Then for each Td chunk, we can use the same P. So: 1. Compute QK -> P_reg[64] 2. Track m, l 3. For each Td chunk of output cols: - Load V tile for this chunk - o_chunk[32] += sum_j P_reg[j] * V[j][d0+dd] 4. After all tiles, normalize and write But we still need to keep O_chunk for all chunks, or write back to global memory and read back. If we process one chunk at a time and for each KV tile, load V for that chunk only, we save V bandwidth but need to recompute P. OK let me think about this differently. The memory bandwidth is our bottleneck. Total memory: - Q: B*Hq*N*D = 4*32*2048*128 = 33.5M bf16 = 67MB (read once) - K, V: B*Hkv*N*D*2 = 4*8*2048*128*2 = 16.7M bf16 = 33.5MB (read once each if cached well, or read multiple times if not) Total: 100.5MB At 3.35TB/s: 0.03ms So memory bandwidth is definitely not the bottleneck if we have good cache behavior. The issue is compute. For the reference, likely using Flash Attention with optimal fused kernels. Our kernel's compute needs to be efficient. What if I use a group of threads per row? For example, 4 threads per row, each handling 32 output columns. Then O_reg is 32 floats per thread. Much less register pressure. Design: - Br = 64 rows per block - Threads per row = 4 (for 128 cols / 4 = 32 cols each) - Total threads = 64 * 4 = 256 threads per block - Threads are organized as 64 rows x 4 cols For QK: - Each row group of 4 threads needs to compute 64 scores - But each thread only needs Q for its row. Not shared within row group. - Actually, each of the 4 threads in a row computes the SAME 64 scores (since QK doesn't depend on output cols). - That's wasteful. Better: 1 thread computes QK and shares with the other 3 via shmem or shfl. - Or: 1 thread computes QK, broadcasts P to the other 3 via shfl, then each does PV for its 32 cols. But this means 3 out of 4 threads are idle during QK. Not ideal. Alternative: Each of the 4 threads computes a portion of the dot product and reduces. - Thread k computes Q[d0:d0+32] · K[j][d0:d0+32] for k=0..3 - Use warp shuffle to sum the 4 partials - Now all 4 threads have the score - Then each does PV for its 32 cols using the shared score This is better! All threads are active. For 64 scores and 4 threads per row: - 64 rows * 4 threads = 256 threads - Each thread computes partial dot for 32 cols - Warp shuffle sum within row group (4 threads) - Broadcast score to all 4 - Each thread accumulates PV for its 32 cols The row groups: threadIdx.x / 4 = row (0..63), threadIdx.x % 4 = col_group (0..3) For warp shuffle: threads in same row but different col_group might not be in same warp. 256 threads = 8 warps. 64 rows / 8 warps = 8 rows per warp. Each warp has 32 threads = 8 rows * 4 col_groups. Yes! 32 threads = 8*4, which matches. So within a warp, there are 8 rows, each with 4 col_group threads. For row shuffle: laneId / 4 gives row within warp (0..7), laneId % 4 gives col_group (0..3). Shuffle across laneId%4 for same row: use mask = ~(3) | (laneId & 3)? No, shfl for threads where (laneId / 4) is the same. For shfl across the 4 threads in the same row within a warp: - Base lane = (laneId / 4) * 4 - Offset = laneId % 4 - We want to reduce across offset 0,1,2,3 for same base. Using __shfl_xor_sync with 1 then 2: - After xor 1: lanes 0<->1, 2<->3 get sum of pairs - After xor 2: lanes 0<->2, 1<->3 get sum of quads Then broadcast with xor 0 (or read from any lane). OK, let me write this properly. Actually, for simplicity, let me just do 1 thread per row but process output in smaller chunks, keeping O_chunk in registers. Let me try a very direct implementation and check correctness. ```python import torch import torch.nn as nn CUDA_SOURCE = r''' #include <cuda_bf16.h> #include <cuda_runtime.h> #include <math.h> extern "C" { __global__ void flash_attn_kernel( const __nv_bfloat16* __restrict__ Q, const __nv_bfloat16* __restrict__ K, const __nv_bfloat16* __restrict__ V, __nv_bfloat16* __restrict__ O, int B, int Hq, int Hkv, int N, int D, float scale ) { int tid = threadIdx.x; int q_row = blockIdx.x * 64 + tid; int hi = blockIdx.y; int bi = blockIdx.z; if (q_row >= N) return; int h_per_kv = Hq / Hkv; int h_kv = hi / h_per_kv; int q_offset = ((bi * Hq + hi) * N + q_row) * D; int kv_offset = ((bi * Hkv + h_kv) * N) * D; int o_offset = q_offset; // Accumulators float o[128]; for (int d = 0; d < D; ++d) o[d] = 0.0f; float m = -1e30f; float l = 0.0f; extern __shared__ __nv_bfloat16 smem[]; __nv_bfloat16* sK = smem; __nv_bfloat16* sV = sK + 64 * D; int num_tiles = (N + 63) / 64; for (int t = 0; t < num_tiles; ++t) { int k_start = t * 64; // Load K and V into shared memory for (int i = tid; i < 64 * D; i += 64) { int r = i / D; int c = i % D; int gidx = kv_offset + (k_start + r) * D + c; bool valid = (k_start + r) < N; sK[i] = valid ? K[gidx] : __float2bfloat16_rn(0.0f); sV[i] = valid ? V[gidx] : __float2bfloat16_rn(0.0f); } __syncthreads(); float s[64]; for (int j = 0; j < 64; ++j) { if (k_start + j < N) { float sum = 0.0f; for (int d = 0; d < D; ++d) { sum += __bfloat162float(Q[q_offset + d]) * __bfloat162float(sK[j * D + d]); } s[j] = sum * scale; } else { s[j] = -1e20f; } } float m_prev = m; for (int j = 0; j < 64; ++j) { m = fmaxf(m, s[j]); } float factor = expf(m_prev - m); for (int d = 0; d < D; ++d) { o[d] *= factor; } l *= factor; float sum_p = 0.0f; for (int j = 0; j < 64; ++j) { s[j] = expf(s[j] - m); sum_p += s[j]; } l += sum_p; for (int d = 0; d < D; ++d) { float acc = 0.0f; for (int j = 0; j < 64; ++j) { acc += s[j] * __bfloat162float(sV[j * D + d]); } o[d] += acc; } __syncthreads(); } float inv_l = 1.0f / l; for (int d = 0; d < D; ++d) { O[o_offset + d] = __float2bfloat16_rn(o[d] * inv_l); } } } ''' class ModelNew(nn.Module): def __init__(self, num_heads, num_kv_heads, head_dim): super().__init__() self.num_heads = num_heads self.num_kv_heads = num_kv_heads self.head_dim = head_dim self.cuda_module = torch.utils.cpp_extension.load_inline( name='flash_attn_simple', cpp_sources='', cuda_sources=CUDA_SOURCE, extra_cuda_cflags=['-O3', '-gencode', 'arch=compute_90,code=sm_90', '-std=c++17', '--maxrregcount=255'], verbose=False, ) def forward(self, q, k, v): batch, num_heads, seq_len, head_dim = q.shape o = torch.empty_like(q) scale = 1.0 / (head_dim ** 0.5) grid = ((seq_len + 63) // 64, num_heads, batch) smem = 64 * head_dim * 2 * 2 # K + V self.cuda_module.flash_attn_kernel[grid, 64, 0, smem]( q, k, v, o, batch, num_heads, self.num_kv_heads, seq_len, head_dim, scale ) return o ``` Wait, D is fixed at 128, so I can use D=128 in the kernel. But if I make it a parameter, it might be more flexible. However, the I/O contract says HEAD_DIM=128. Also, I need to make sure Q is read correctly. In the loop: ```cuda sum += __bfloat162float(Q[q_offset + d]) * __bfloat162float(sK[j * D + d]); ``` Q is read in the inner loop. Each thread reads Q[128] for each of the 64 keys, so Q is read 64 times. But q_offset is fixed per thread, so Q for this thread's row is read from global memory. Given L1 cache, this should be cached after the first read. Alternatively, I could load Q to registers first. Let me load Q to registers to be sure: ```cuda float q_reg[128]; for (int d = 0; d < D; ++d) q_reg[d] = __bfloat162float(Q[q_offset + d]); ``` Then use q_reg in the inner loop. This adds 128 registers but ensures Q is read once. With 64 threads per block and each having q_reg[128], that's 128 floats = 512 bytes per thread. Register file per block = 64 * 512 = 32KB, or 8192 registers total per block. At 255 per thread = 16320 registers available. 128 for q + 128 for o + 64 for s = 320 registers. Over limit. So I should NOT load Q to registers. Just read from global memory repeatedly and rely on L1. Let me remove q_reg and read from Q directly. Updated inner loop: ```cuda for (int j = 0; j < 64; ++j) { if (k_start + j < N) { float sum = 0.0f; for (int d = 0; d < D; ++d) { sum += __bfloat162float(Q[q_offset + d]) * __bfloat162float(sK[j * D + d]); } s[j] = sum * scale; } else { s[j] = -1e20f; } } ``` For the PV accumulation: ```cuda for (int d = 0; d < D; ++d) { float acc = 0.0f; for (int j = 0; j < 64; ++j) { acc += s[j] * __bfloat162float(sV[j * D + d]); } o[d] += acc; } ``` o is 128 floats = 128 registers s is 64 floats = 64 registers Plus loop indices, temps: ~20 registers Total: ~212 registers. Within 255 limit. Good! But wait, o stays in registers for the entire kernel duration. And s stays too. That's 192 registers. Plus the compiler generates more for loop unrolling or address calculations. It might spill, or it might just work. With --maxrregcount=255, the compiler will try hard. Actually, I realize that reading Q[q_offset + d] in the inner j loop means for each j, we do 128 loads from Q. For 64 j's, that's 8192 loads from Q. But q_offset + d is the same address sequence for all j, so L1 cache should make subsequent loads free after the first iteration. Let me also consider vectorized loads for sV in the accumulation loop. Since sV is in shared memory, we can load in pairs: ```cuda for (int d = 0; d < D; d += 2) { float acc0 = 0.0f, acc1 = 0.0f; for (int j = 0; j < 64; ++j) { acc0 += s[j] * __bfloat162float(sV[j * D + d]); acc1 += s[j] * __bfloat162float(sV[j * D + d + 1]); } o[d] += acc0; o[d+1] += acc1; } ``` This doubles the register usage temporarily but is fine.
